<figcaption><p>Canny detector and sigma parameter value settings. <strong>a</strong>) Image acquired by applying canny edge detection; <strong>b</strong>) sigma = 1; <strong>c</strong>) sigma = 2; <strong>d</strong>) sigma = 2.8; <strong>e</strong>) sigma = 3. The value of sigma at index (<strong>d</strong>) represents the optimal value found during the pre-processing phase for training ML models on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq88"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq89"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq90"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/fe0b0df886f0/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq90.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq91"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/d9aea7067aff/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq91.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig6"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12069673_41598_2025_98356_Fig6_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/78785f544fef/41598_2025_98356_Fig6_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO6" height="149" width="792" alt="Fig. 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Samples of real spiral images from augmented dataset (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq92"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq23.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>) representing hand-drawn spirals of healthy and Parkinson’s classes.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par34">The key difference between the original and Canny-processed datasets lies in the preprocessing applied to the images. The Canny edge detection algorithm detects edges within the spiral drawings, and Hessian filtering is then used to sharpen the image by emphasizing the ridges. This preprocessing enhances the structural details, making the features more defined. In contrast, the original dataset remains intact, preserving all the raw image data without any enhancement or noise reduction. The Canny-processed dataset provides a modified version of the images that highlights specific features relevant for analysis, while the original dataset retains its full spectrum of features, including both noise and structural elements. These different datasets contribute to the analysis by enabling a comparison of model performance using original and edge-detected features.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Figa"><h4 class="obj_head">Algorithm 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12069673_41598_2025_98356_Figa_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/c865ad2a98b7/41598_2025_98356_Figa_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO7" height="298" width="669" alt="Algorithm 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Figa/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Data augmentation.</p></figcaption></figure><section id="Sec17"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Feature extraction</h4>
<p id="Par36">This study’s novelty relies on extracting the low-level features from the four datasets. The study employs manual feature extraction methods implementing statistical attributes extracted from images from each prepared dataset. The study employs a sequential feature extraction process while considering the two different approaches of edge detection: a) Histogram-of-oriented gradients (HoG) and b) Canny edge detection. As explained in the prior subsection, the first approach prepares HoG images by subjecting images in <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq101"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> to the HoG transform while the second approach implements a canny edge detector with a hessian filter to generate canny images for the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq102"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/5438ccc92554/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq28.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> or <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq103"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. The datasets <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq104"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq105"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> input to the data augmentation algorithm generate the augmented datasets <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq106"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq107"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. The handcrafted feature extraction process required computing eleven statistical features vectors – <strong>mean, standard deviation, skewness, kurtosis, energy, power, median, variance, minimum, maximum, and rms</strong>; all annotated by numerals 0 through 10, respectively and estimated using Python packages – <em>numpy</em> and <em>scipy</em>.<em>stats</em>. As such, the feature space for training ML models can be represented by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq108"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bb10c3c9f464/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq108.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. The Recursive Feature Selection (RFS) algorithm enabled finding the best possible features to generate a feature set, such as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq109"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/9232d9c3da6f/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq109.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> from the realized feature space. The study uses the same convention throughout this article.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Figb"><h5 class="obj_head">Algorithm 2.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12069673_41598_2025_98356_Figb_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/94305c2add2b/41598_2025_98356_Figb_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO8" height="293" width="796" alt="Algorithm 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Figb/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Canny edge detection with hessian filtering.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par38">The selection of the eleven statistical feature was specifically tailored to extract meaningful insights from spiral images in the dataset. Spiral patterns provide unique structural and textural characteristics indicative of fine motor skills, often impaired in Parkinson’s disease. Features such as mean and median capture the overall intensity distribution within the spirals, providing a baseline for comparing normal and pathological patterns. Standard deviation and variance highlight the variability in stroke pressure and consistency, often more pronounced in Parkinson’s spirals due to tremors. Higher-order statistics like skewness and kurtosis detect asymmetry and peak sharpness in the patterns, reflecting the irregularities typical of Parkinson’s handwriting. Energy, power, and rms are texture-oriented features that quantify the concentration and contrast within the spiral strokes, emphasizing differences in drawing control and force. Minimum and maximum values define the intensity range, capturing boundary variations in the strokes that can signal tremor severity or rigidity. These features were chosen because they effectively summarize the structural irregularities and dynamic variations inherent in spiral drawings, making them highly relevant for distinguishing Healthy versus Parkinson’s cases. Their selection is grounded in both their proven applicability to similar medical image analyses and their computational efficiency for robust feature extraction.</p></section><section id="Sec18"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Feature selection</h4>
<p id="Par39">The Recursive Feature Elimination (RFE) process is a feature selection technique that recursively removes features from the dataset and builds a model to evaluate the performance after each elimination. Initially, the algorithm fits a model using all available features and ranks them based on their importance. Features with the highest importance are kept, while those with the lowest importance (i.e., the lowest ranking value) are eliminated. This method ensures that the final feature set is efficient and effective for training the model. The process is repeated until the desired number of features is selected. The RFE function in Python programming language ranks features inversely, where lower-ranked features are deemed more important, retaining features with lower rank values. In our implementation as shown in algorithm <a href="#Figc" class="usa-link">3</a>, the optimal feature set was determined by selecting features ranked lower in the RFE process, indicating higher importance for the model. Specifically, a threshold rank value of 2 was set, and features with a rank value of 2 or lower were retained, while others were discarded. This threshold was chosen based on balancing model complexity and performance, ensuring that only the most relevant features were used, reducing the risk of overfitting. RFE is chosen over Principal Component Analysis (PCA) because, while PCA effectively reduces dimensionality and removes redundant features, it can obscure the interpretability of the original features. PCA transforms the data into a set of orthogonal components, which may not correspond directly to the original features, making it harder to interpret the model’s behaviour regarding the actual variables. Additionally, PCA assumes linear relationships between features, which may not capture complex, non-linear relationships present in the data. On the other hand, RFE directly evaluates feature importance based on model performance, allowing us to retain features that contribute most to predictive power while preserving their interpretability and relevance to the task at hand.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Figc"><h5 class="obj_head">Algorithm 3.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12069673_41598_2025_98356_Figc_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/13034debf90d/41598_2025_98356_Figc_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO9" height="199" width="668" alt="Algorithm 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Figc/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Feature ranking using RFE.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par41">The study implemented the RFS algorithm for selecting the best possible <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq110"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/1c9849dc1f28/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq110.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> out of statistical features as input to the RFS algorithm for training the models. RFS ranked features with scores providing a subset of features; however, the observation here is that different models require different <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq111"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/1c9849dc1f28/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq110.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> for achieving high prediction accuracy. Intuitively, the need to find a <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq112"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/c785c361cecc/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq112.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> enabling all models to have almost the same prediction accuracy was observed. The study presents a method depicted in algorithm <a href="#Figd" class="usa-link">4</a> that combines RFS determined optimal feature vectors for creating the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq113"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/c785c361cecc/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq112.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. Observations show that the feature vectors in <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq114"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/1c9849dc1f28/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq110.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> individually determined for models by the RFS showcase common feature vectors. Comprising <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq115"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/c785c361cecc/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq112.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> of feature vectors common to all individually determined feature sets for different models serves as the best <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq116"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/c785c361cecc/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq112.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, generalizing model prediction accuracies across the range of models used in the study. However, adding other feature vectors common to some models, although only in some, improves the corresponding models’ accuracy. In addition, the observations show that only the most common features for small datasets enable models to achieve prediction accuracies higher than the accuracies resulting from the large dataset.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Figd"><h5 class="obj_head">Algorithm 4.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12069673_41598_2025_98356_Figd_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/6f5845039c04/41598_2025_98356_Figd_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO10" height="427" width="796" alt="Algorithm 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Figd/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Feature space generator.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec19"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Model training and performance evaluation</h4>
<p id="Par43">A review of the literature reveals that a 10-fold cross-validation improves ML model performance; therefore, the study evaluates classifiers’ performances using the 10-fold stratified cross-validation technique<sup><a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>,<a href="#CR34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR34">34</a>,<a href="#CR36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR36">36</a>,<a href="#CR42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR42">42</a>,<a href="#CR43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR43">43</a>,<a href="#CR47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR47">47</a></sup>. All of the classifiers are evaluated using stratified <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq117"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/7479f09dc4a3/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq117.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> cross-validation. By randomly dividing the data into <em>k</em> equal-sized groups or folds while maintaining the same number of samples per class, the method generates <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq118"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/5aac343563d8/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq118.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> training and a test group<sup><a href="#CR66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR66">66</a></sup>. The models are trained/tested ten times in each cross-validation iteration. The model training is based on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq119"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/c785c361cecc/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq112.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> specific to each model.</p>
<p id="Par44">As mentioned in the previous section, the study employs <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq120"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/e0743864e23a/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq120.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> StratifiedKFold cross-validation to validate the trained models. Nine models are trained in four folds on four different datasets <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq121"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/d842bb7a4d77/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq121.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq122"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. All nine models employ default calibration settings provided by the Python programming language.</p></section></section><section id="Sec20"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Performance evaluation metrics</h3>
<p id="Par45">Machine-learning algorithms are evaluated using a variety of metrics. The study employs the following performance metrics to evaluate the performances of employed ML classifiers. The metrics used in the study are accuracy, Receiver Operating Characteristic Curve (RoC) curves, F-score, and Area Under the Curve (AUC) confusion matrix. The bias-variance plots showcase the complexities of models on the number of features employed for training the models. In addition, the study also reports the memory footprint and prediction time for each of the employed models.</p>
<section id="Sec21"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Accuracy (ACC)</h4>
<p id="Par46">Accuracy (ACC) is given by the Eq. (<a href="#Equ14" class="usa-link">14</a>) and defines the proportion of correct prediction of a given condition<sup><a href="#CR67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR67">67</a>,<a href="#CR68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR68">68</a></sup>.</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ14"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/a7e56d15f043/41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ14.gif" loading="lazy" height="33" width="200" alt="graphic file with name 41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ14.gif"></td>
<td class="label">14</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Where <em>TP</em> represents the number of true positives, <em>TN</em> represents the number of true negatives, <em>FP</em> represents the number of false positives, and <em>FN</em> represents the number of false negatives.</p></section><section id="Sec22"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Area under curve (AUC)</h4>
<p id="Par47">A commonly used performance evaluation approach that applies to binary classification problems. A classifier’s AUC estimates the probability score, defining that the model under evaluation would rank a randomly chosen positive rather than a randomly chosen negative example. Equation (<a href="#Equ15" class="usa-link">15</a>) provides the AUC, or area under the curve<sup><a href="#CR69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR69">69</a></sup>. The model performs better at differentiating between people with and without tremor symptoms the higher the AUC is in a range from 0 to 1.</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ15"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfe020b20f43/41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ15.gif" loading="lazy" height="44" width="172" alt="graphic file with name 41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ15.gif"></td>
<td class="label">15</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Estimation of AUC requires the definition of the following two parameters:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc"><li><p id="Par48">True Positive Rate (Sensitivity or TPR): TPR is the percentage of positive data points that are accurately interpreted as positive compared to all positive data points<sup><a href="#CR29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR29">29</a>,<a href="#CR68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR68">68</a>–<a href="#CR70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR70">70</a></sup>. TRP is expressed by the equation eq(<a href="#Equ16" class="usa-link">16</a>). </p></li></ul>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ16"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/6ac352452f97/41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ16.gif" loading="lazy" height="38" width="136" alt="graphic file with name 41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ16.gif"></td>
<td class="label">16</td>
</tr></table>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc"><li><p id="Par49">True Negative Rate (Specificity or TNR): TNR is the percentage of positive data points that are accurately interpreted as negative compared to all negative data points<sup><a href="#CR29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR29">29</a>,<a href="#CR68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR68">68</a>–<a href="#CR70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR70">70</a></sup>. TRP is expressed by the equation eq(<a href="#Equ17" class="usa-link">17</a>). </p></li></ul>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ17"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/d6adbc0a7634/41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ17.gif" loading="lazy" height="38" width="140" alt="graphic file with name 41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ17.gif"></td>
<td class="label">17</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Further, the AUC is computed as follows:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ18"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/5e1b472a7d5d/41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ18.gif" loading="lazy" height="37" width="194" alt="graphic file with name 41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ18.gif"></td>
<td class="label">18</td>
</tr></table></section><section id="Sec23"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">F1 score</h4>
<p id="Par50">The harmonic mean of the precision and recall gives the formula for the conventional F1 score expressed by the Eq. (<a href="#Equ19" class="usa-link">19</a>). F1-score. An F-score of 1 indicates a perfect model<sup><a href="#CR29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR29">29</a>,<a href="#CR68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR68">68</a>–<a href="#CR70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR70">70</a></sup>.</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ19"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/d244ecd3e3b3/41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ19.gif" loading="lazy" height="44" width="187" alt="graphic file with name 41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ19.gif"></td>
<td class="label">19</td>
</tr></table></section><section id="Sec24"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Receiver Operating Characteristic curve (RoC) Plot</h4>
<p id="Par51">A RoC curve is a graph that depicts the performance of a classification model across all categorization levels. The curve compares the TPR and False Positive Rate (FPR) expressed by the Eqs. (<a href="#Equ16" class="usa-link">16</a>) and (<a href="#Equ20" class="usa-link">20</a>), respectively. The FPR is the percentage of negative data points incorrectly interpreted as positive compared to all negative data points.</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ20"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/8e6e3a9c3a40/41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ20.gif" loading="lazy" height="38" width="139" alt="graphic file with name 41598_2025_98356_Article_Equ20.gif"></td>
<td class="label">20</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The TPR and the FPR values range from [0, 1]. Estimation of FPR and TPR at distinct threshold values, such as (0.00, 0.02, 0.04, ..., 1.00) can be plotted graphically, whereas the AUC defines the area under the curve of a plot of FPR vs TRP at different points in the range [0, 1]. Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a> shows the typical values of AUC and their corresponding interpretation.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section></section></section><section id="Sec25"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results and discussion</h2>
<p id="Par52">Training the models using a fourfold approach implementing four different datasets generates four different types of outputs that showcase performance at an individual level specific to every model. The next subsections showcase the experiment results and provide a detailed discussion.</p>
<section id="Sec26"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">ML model training on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq123"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq124"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>
</h3>
<p id="Par53">The correlation matrix formulated using a combination of various extracted features enabled identifying the correlation between the different feature vectors. The correlation matrix shown in Fig. <a href="#Fig7" class="usa-link">7</a> represents three feature vectors only, and a combination of these three feature vectors represents the feature space seen as input by the nine ML models. The RFS method removed the highly correlating features while generating feature sets specific to each model. However, a feature set enabling similar prediction accuracies by all nine models is selected. Therefore, feature set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq125"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/573fb7c9fc9f/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq125.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> represents the best possible feature set among all the RFS-generated features selected for training the models. In addition, eliminating the features 4 and 6 reduced the feature space for the small sized dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq126"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig7"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/6b95a7979120/41598_2025_98356_Fig7_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO11" height="489" width="642" alt="Fig. 7"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Correlation matrix defining correlation of feature combinations handcrafted from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq127"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par54">The correlation matrix in Fig. <a href="#Fig8" class="usa-link">8</a> enabled finding the feature space for the augmented dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq129"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. Removing additional features other than those removed by the RFS results in lower prediction accuracies by the models. As such, the RFS generated features space comprising <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq130"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bd7735207c9b/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq130.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> enabled models’ training and ensured similar prediction accuracies. While some models show the same behaviour on the reduced feature space, a decrease in memory footprint and prediction times of the ML models trained is observed (see Figs. <a href="#Fig19" class="usa-link">19</a> and <a href="#Fig20" class="usa-link">20</a>).</p>

<figcaption><p>Memory footprint of models trained on dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq168"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p></figcaption></figure><section id="Sec27"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Model calibration</h4>
<p id="Par55">As mentioned in the prior section, the study employs un-calibrated models with default configuration settings. Expected model behaviour showcases a non-alignment with the perfect calibration line when models are trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq131"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. However, observations show an alignment of generated calibration curves with the perfect curve for models trained on a large-size dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq132"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. The results reveal that large-size datasets automatically calibrate the models to some extent; however, they remain non-aligned to the ideal perfect calibration model state curve. Figures <a href="#Fig9" class="usa-link">9</a> and <a href="#Fig10" class="usa-link">10</a> show the calibration curves for the models trained on small and large datasets whilst considering the feature space size, respectively. The models trained on large datasets showcase curves nearly aligning with the perfect model calibration line although not exhibiting perfect <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq133"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/712d60128348/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq133.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> curves that typically result with calibrated models. The study highlights that calibrating models before training may improve the prediction accuracies of the models further. Nonetheless, the task has been left over for future work.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig9"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 9.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12069673_41598_2025_98356_Fig9_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/1cd2d20d2e1b/41598_2025_98356_Fig9_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO13" height="271" width="792" alt="Fig. 9"></a></p>

</h3>
<section id="Sec30"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Model ROC</h4>
<p id="Par57">The ROC curves demonstrate how different models behave towards the task of identifying the handwritten spiral drawings of healthy and PD patients. Figure <a href="#Fig12" class="usa-link">12</a> shows the ROC plots for the models trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq140"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. Observing the results reveals that the models DT and ADB classify spiral drawing of healthy and PD patients with an AUC of 0.92; the highest AUC compared to other AUC of other classifiers. RF and GB show the AUC values of 0.90 and 0.91, respectively, thereby demonstrating equal competitiveness with the DT and ADB that attain the highest value of AUC among all the classifiers. While the other models reveal AUC values ranging from 0.85 to 0.87, the LR model remains the worst classifier to distinguish between the two classes.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig12"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 12.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12069673_41598_2025_98356_Fig12_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/10660e57e8e2/41598_2025_98356_Fig12_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO16" height="287" width="791" alt="Fig. 12"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig12/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>ROC curves for models trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq141"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par58">A significant increase can be observed in the AUC for the models trained using augmented dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq143"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. As shown in Fig. <a href="#Fig13" class="usa-link">13</a>, out of nine, the AUC value for six models is above 0.9 while the AUC values for NB, LR, and DT are 0.83, 0.86, and 0.88. The observations reveal that classification results improve with large datasets consisting of healthy and PD patients’ images. Amongst the nine models, the models capable of attaining the highest AUC value of 0.97 are RF, XGB, and SVM.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig13"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 13.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12069673_41598_2025_98356_Fig13_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/a75a8e45c0e0/41598_2025_98356_Fig13_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO17" height="287" width="791" alt="Fig. 13"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig13/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>ROC curves for models trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq142"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec31"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Model learning</h4>
<p id="Par59">The learning curves, as shown in Fig. <a href="#Fig14" class="usa-link">14</a>, display the training accuracies for models trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq144"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> range between 73% to 90% approximately. Logistic regression and Naive Bayes model show decreasing accuracies below 75% whereas the models Extreme Gradient Boosting)(XGB), Random Forest (RF) and Support Vector Machines (SVM) show nearly mid-level prediction accuracies reaching up to 80% approximately. Above the mid-level, however, lower than the highest level, the K-Nearest Neighbors(KNN) and AdaBoost (ADB) models exhibit prediction accuracies reaching approximately 89%. Only the Gradient Boosting(GB) and Decision tree (DT) models predict with the highest prediction accuracy of 91.7%, approximately. The average accuracy range of models is 83%. Likewise, the learning curves shown in Fig. <a href="#Fig15" class="usa-link">15</a> display the training accuracies for models trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq145"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> range between 78% to 92% approximately. Naive Bayes and Logistic regression models show 77% and 78% accuracy, respectively. Only the ADB exhibits the mid-range of accuracy of 83%. The SVM, GB, and DT showcase nearly the same accuracy of 87%, approximately. Only the Decision tree (DT) model predicts with the highest prediction accuracy of 91.7%, approximately. Overall, the average accuracy range of models is 83%. The RF and XBG predict with 90% accuracy; however, the KNN model achieves the highest prediction accuracy of approximately 92%. The overall average accuracy of all the models is 86%, thereby showing a significant increase in accuracy by 3%.</p>

<figcaption><p>Learning curves for models trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq147"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec32"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Model confusion matrix</h4>
<p id="Par60">Confusion matrices in Fig. <a href="#Fig16" class="usa-link">16</a> show the estimated values of TP, TN, FP, and FN for the nine models. The confusion matrices framed on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq148"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> show that nearly all the classifiers perform well, except the LR model, which shows a higher account of FPs and FNs. The models DT, GB, KNN, and ADB showcase consideration classification in terms of TPs and TNs, while the models RF, XGB, and SVM exhibit nearly medium classification levels with misclassification. The DT model shows the lowest FPs and FNs and the highest TPs and TNs among all the nine models. This implies DT classifies the spiral curves of healthy and PD patients, comprising a small dataset for classification. In contrast, the confusion matrices for the models trained on large dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq149"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> show significant improvements in the nine models’ estimated TP, TN, FP, and FN values. Observation reveals that the NB model shows the highest account of FPs and FNs despite the increase in the size of the dataset. The next classifier to showcase declining performance on the increased size of the dataset is the LR. The models DT, GB, SVM and ADB show the number of FPs and FNs higher than the FPs and FNs for the RF and KNN models and represent models with mid-range misclassifications. However, the KNN model shows the lowest number of FPs and FNs and the highest TPs and TNs among all the nine models. This implies that increasing the size of the dataset for the KNN model increases its classification accuracy on the image dataset consisting of spiral curves of healthy and PD patients.</p>

<figcaption><p>Confusion matrices–results of 10-fold cross validation results on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq150"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq151"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. Labels <em>H</em> and <em>P</em> denote the Healthy and Parkinson’s class of patients, respectively.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec33"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Bias-variance trade-off</h4>
<p id="Par61">Bias and variance are the two sources of error that affect model performance. Bias-variance curves are a valuable visualization tool for analyzing the trade-off between bias and variance in ML models. The study considers elucidating the construction and interpretation of bias-variance curves essential. The study attempts to explore the trade-off between bias and variance for the nine ML models. Figures <a href="#Fig17" class="usa-link">17</a> and <a href="#Fig18" class="usa-link">18</a> show the bias-variance curves for models trained on datasets <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq152"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq153"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. The curves in Fig. <a href="#Fig17" class="usa-link">17</a> depict the bias-variance for models trained on the combinations of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq154"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/573fb7c9fc9f/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq154.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. The different feature combinations are generated for tracing the bias-variance curves representing the models’ behaviour on different feature sets. The results reveal that the models trained on a low count of features showcase a near-perfect trade-off between bias and variance. These low count of feature vectors showcase unstablized trends in the means squared error (MSE) for all models except the LR and SVM model with optimal trade-off – stabilized MSE. Other models showcase varying behaviours, although the bias and variance trade-offs exist at certain combinations of features. This implies that the bias-variance trade-off is dynamic in nature and depends on the model type and possible feature combinations employed for classifying the spiral curve of healthy and PD patients.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig17"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 17.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12069673_41598_2025_98356_Fig17_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/a0655bf50a9f/41598_2025_98356_Fig17_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO21" height="390" width="792" alt="Fig. 17"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig17/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Bias-variance curves for models trained on dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq155"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig18"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 18.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12069673_41598_2025_98356_Fig18_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/41b25eefdba2/41598_2025_98356_Fig18_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO22" height="405" width="791" alt="Fig. 18"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig18/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Bias-variance curves for models trained on dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq156"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par62">On the other hand, feature sets handcrafted out of augmented dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq157"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> showcase trade-offs at specific combinations of features only. As depicted in Fig. <a href="#Fig18" class="usa-link">18</a>, different models exhibit trade-offs at certain specific feature sets. This implies that finding a feature set enabling a perfect trade-off between bias and variance exhibited by the models still requires further investigation. Moreover, the observation reveals that means squared errors for individual models show elevation compared to models trained to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq158"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. The RF, GB, and XGB showcase declining bias and variance trends; however, with increased MSE. The other models also showcase similar behaviour, except the SVM showcasing a stabilizing trends for the bias, variance and MSE. Analytical works explaining and unveiling such relations are possible candidates for continued investigation in future studies. Therefore, the nine models classifying the handwritten, drawn spiral images by healthy and PD patients need further investigation to ensure bias-variance trade-off could be analyzed at individual levels and then generalized.</p></section><section id="Sec34"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Model memory footprint</h4>
<p id="Par63">Efficient memory management in ML models is critical, particularly when deploying models in resource-constrained environments, mobile devices, or cloud-based systems with limited memory capacity. Researchers and practitioners, therefore, must seek insight into the memory requirements of the models and explore techniques to reduce the memory footprint while maintaining performance. The study presents ML models’ memory footprint for classifying healthy and PD patients. Figures <a href="#Fig19" class="usa-link">19</a> and <a href="#Fig20" class="usa-link">20</a> represent the memory footprints of models trained on features combinations derived from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq159"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/89288220bc0f/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq159.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq160"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/1fc2c2ed85f6/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq160.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, respectively. The RF and ADB models depict higher memory requirements than those in Fig. <a href="#Fig19" class="usa-link">19</a>. Expect the RF and ADB models, a maximum of three feature vectors employed for training all the models on spiral image dataset depict lower memory requirements ranging between 1KB to 7 KB. While the second-highest model, ADB, showcases a memory requirement of approximately 33 KB, the RF model showcases the highest memory requirement of approximately 61 KB. Constant memory requirements could be observed for other models; however, KNN and SVM show elevated requirements with an increase in the number of feature counts of two ([2, 3]) to three ([1, 2, 3]) features. Observation reveals that some models showcase constant memory requirements, whereas others exhibit increasing memory requirements and increased feature space.</p>
<p id="Par64">Comparing the memory requirements of models trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq161"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq162"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> showcases the various models’ varied differences in memory size requirements. Figure <a href="#Fig20" class="usa-link">20</a> depicts the memory usage by various models. Observing the memory footprint reveals that some of the models trained on augmented dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq163"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> showcase elevation in memory requirements while the other models exhibit the same memory requirement as observed in models trained on small size dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq164"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. The models GB, XGB, LR, DT, and NB trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq165"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> exhibit memory space requirements ranging between 1KB to 7.5KB approximately, while models ADB, RF, KNN, and SVM showcase increasing memory space requirement when trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq166"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. As depicted in Fig. <a href="#Fig20" class="usa-link">20</a>, the SVM memory requirements are dynamic and rely mainly on different combinations of features. However, all other models, except the kNN and GB, showcase a consistent memory requirement across the entire feature space. Observing the memory footprint, in general, reveals that for some models, the memory requirements are independent of the feature space; however, some models showcase an increase in the memory requirement with the increase in the number of feature vectors employed for training the models. This study recommends selecting model features that ensure consistency in model size with low memory requirements to meet the demand for resource-constrained devices. Edge-computing devices with memory constraints could benefit from this explainable feature of training the models since model sizes suitable for memory-constrained devices can be easily figured out. This study focuses on classifying images of hand-drawn spirals by healthy and PD patients based on ML models with low memory requirements only.</p></section><section id="Sec35"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Prediction time</h4>
<p id="Par65">Prediction time typically refers to the time a trained model takes to produce a prediction or output for a given input. Model prediction time is particularly crucial in edge computing environments. Since edge devices often have limited computational resources compared to powerful cloud servers, model prediction time becomes critical. In edge computing, devices and sensors at the network edge collect and process data, and ML models may be deployed directly on these edge devices. This study lists the prediction times exhibited by nine models employed for classifying the hand-drawn spirals of the PID. Figures <a href="#Fig21" class="usa-link">21</a> and <a href="#Fig22" class="usa-link">22</a> illustrate the prediction time of these models employed in this study. The results show that a few of the models showcase higher prediction times compared to the others. For instance, the ML models – RF, XGB, ADB, and GB take <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq169"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/d04dc1bf4cde/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq169.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> to generate predictions on the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq170"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq19.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> while trained on the same dataset, the ML models – SVM, LR, NB, KNN, and DT can generate fast predictions <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq171"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/6c56fdc4096a/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq171.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. Moreover, the ML models trained on the augmented dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq172"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, being the large sized dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq173"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq19.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, showcase different prediction behaviours. Some models retain the prediction rates while others showcase variations, either increasing with the size of the dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq174"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq19.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> or even the opposite. However, all the ML models showcase an incline in their prediction times with the increase in the size of the dataset.</p>

</h3>
<p id="Par66">The correlation matrix shown in Fig. <a href="#Fig23" class="usa-link">23</a> represents five feature vectors, and the combination of these five feature vectors represents the feature space seen as input by the nine ML models. Again, the RFS removes the highly correlating features here while identifying feature sets specific to each model. Following the same approach employed for dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq179"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, the feature set enabling similar prediction accuracies by all nine models is selected. Therefore, feature set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq180"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/4b26be02f824/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq180.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> represents the best possible feature set among all the RFS-generated features to train the models. In contrast to the prior dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq181"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, reducing the feature space by eliminating features results in low model prediction accuracy. As such, the feature space generated by the RFS trains the models and reduces the number of features impacting model accuracies.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig23"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 23.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/7b4d5cfb028a/41598_2025_98356_Fig23_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO27" height="489" width="642" alt="Fig. 23"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig23/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Correlation matrix defining correlation of feature combinations handcrafted from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq182"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par67">Training models to achieve an optimum accuracy level required deriving a new feature space from the augmented dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq184"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> instead of using the feature space derived from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq185"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> since model prediction scores remained low in the models trained on feature space derived from the dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq186"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. The correlation matrix in Fig. <a href="#Fig24" class="usa-link">24</a> shows the correlation within the feature space derived from the augmented dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq187"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. Observing the RFS-generated feature space consisting of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq188"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/47a07a736a31/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq188.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq189"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> reveals that the models’ prediction accuracies remain the same. The <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq190"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/47a07a736a31/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq188.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> is a feature set handcrafted from the augmented dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq191"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. The feature space derived from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq192"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> for training the models ensured that models achieved nearly the same prediction accuracy. This implies that applying canny edge detection to the images of hand-drawn spirals by healthy and PD patients lowers the performance of models. Moreover, the feature space derived from augmented datasets remains the same, implying that augmenting the dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq193"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> ensures consistency in the feature space. This in turn, enables models to source predictions at the same level whilst performing classification on healthy and PD patients.</p>

<figcaption><p>Correlation matrix defining correlation of feature combinations handcrafted from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq183"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p></figcaption></figure><section id="Sec37"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Model calibration</h4>
<p id="Par68">The study employs un-calibrated models with default configuration settings, as mentioned in the prior sections. Expected model behaviours showcase a non-alignment with the perfect calibration line when the nine models are trained on dataset feature space derived from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq194"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. Compared to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq195"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, the results showcase alignments of models’ calibration curves to some extent with the perfect calibration line while the models are trained with the augmented dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq196"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. Again, an augmented dataset does not lead to ideal <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq197"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/04cab43e5678/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq197.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> curves that result from calibrated models.</p>
<p id="Par69">For observing the difference, the study shows that large-size datasets automatically calibrate the models to some extent. However, images of hand-drawn spirals by healthy and PD patients, when subjected to pre-processing with a canny edge detector, lower the prediction accuracies of ML models. In addition, the models trained on small-size datasets exhibit model calibration curves that remain non-aligned to the ideal perfect calibration model state curve, implying the model prediction accuracies eventually decrease with un-calibrated models. The study limits the scope to un-calibrated models only; however, the task of realizing the classification of calibrated models is left over for future work. Figures <a href="#Fig25" class="usa-link">25</a> and <a href="#Fig26" class="usa-link">26</a> show the calibration curves for the models trained on small and large datasets used to derive the feature space size, respectively. The models trained on large dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq200"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> showcase curves nearly aligning with the perfect model calibration line, nonetheless not exhibiting the perfect <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq201"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/712d60128348/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq133.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> curves resulting in calibrated models. The study highlights that calibrating models before training may improve the prediction accuracies of the models further. Estimating the calibrated performance has been left over for future works.</p>

</h3>
<section id="Sec40"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Model ROC</h4>
<p id="Par71">Figures <a href="#Fig28" class="usa-link">28</a> and <a href="#Fig29" class="usa-link">29</a> show the ROC plots for the models trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq206"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq207"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. The <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq208"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> results show that the models classify spiral drawings of healthy and PD patients with an AUC value ranging between 0.81 to 0.85. All classifiers show nearly the same AUC values; however, only the DT decreases AUC levelling at 0.78. RF and KNN show the same value of AUC 0.85 while the AUC values for the models NB, LR, XGB, GB, SVM, and ADB remain lower than RF and KNN. RF and KNN show the highest AUC of 0.85. The models trained on augmented dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq209"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> depict increased AUC values. Classifiers RF, GB, XGB, and SVM show an increase in the AUC values, reaching up to the AUC of 0.89. However, the AUC for DT remains low even if the size of the dataset increases. NB, LR and KNN almost share the same AUC of 0.85, 0.87 and 0.87, respectively. The ADB just follows the models with the highest AUC values. The observation reveals that datasets <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq210"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq211"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> show consistent AUC for all models except LR. The AUC for both the datasets almost follow each other despite different. This implies that the canny dataset showcases consistent performances across the range of models.</p>

<p id="Par72">Figure <a href="#Fig31" class="usa-link">31</a> shows the learning curves for models trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq216"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> range and the level of cross-validation accuracies of various models that range between 71% to 82% approximately. The KNN model shows the highest accuracy of 82%, while XGB shows a lower accuracy level than the other models. The SVM and ADB almost show the same cross-validation accuracy. Training accuracies almost remain above 80% for all the models. However, for NB and SVM, it lowers gradually with increasing count of samples. Figure <a href="#Fig32" class="usa-link">32</a> shows the training and cross-validation accuracies for models trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq217"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. The training accuracy for all ML models is high and ranges between 78% to 100%, approximately. However, the cross-validation accuracy range compared to training accuracy is low and ranges between 73% to 83%. While the highest cross-validation accuracies are observed in RF, GB, XGB, and SVM, the LR, DT, KNN, and ADB models showcase lower cross-validation performance. The NB shows the lowest cross-validation accuracy among all the nine models. Observing both the learning curves for models trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq218"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/12704d766171/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq2.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq219"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, a performance improvement in cross-validation accuracy can be observed. However, the performance stays behind the cross-validation performance exhibited by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq220"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq221"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. This implies canny edge detection; although it ensures consistency in the models’ performance, however levels down the cross-validation accuracy of the models at the same time.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig31"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 31.</h5>

<figcaption><p>Learning curves for models trained on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq223"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec42"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Model confusion matrix</h4>
<p id="Par73">The confusion matrices in Fig. <a href="#Fig30" class="usa-link">30</a> show the estimated TP, TN, FP, and FN values for the nine models. The TP for all the models is nearly identical except for RF and XGB. Out of 36 image samples of hand-drawn spiral images, 29 to 31 are correctly identified by the classifiers. However, the models show differences in identifying the TNs. LR, DT, GB, and XGB showcase accounts of TNs lower in count than TNs identified by RF, KNN, SVM, and ADB. The model exhibiting the lowest TNs is NB, with just 21 samples. The confusion matrix shown in Fig. <a href="#Fig30" class="usa-link">30</a> for the models trained on dataset <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq224"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/341843b529cb/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq6.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> shows that nearly all the models showcase consistent behaviour in identifying the TPs and TNs, except with some minor differences. However, the NB model shows the lowest TNs among all the nine models. All nine models showcase the TP and TN rates. This implies the augmented spiral dataset based on canny edge detection enables all models to behave consistently, providing the same level of accuracy across the entire range of employed models in the study. While this might be a trade-off representing a suitable dataset for training the models for equal performance, the accuracy levels of models are lower than model cross-validation accuracies enabled by the datasets <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq225"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/bfdda8b37d89/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq1.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq226"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/af6016d2d5ab/41598_2025_98356_Article_IEq4.gif" loading="lazy" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. This shows that a canny edge detection-based dataset can generalize model behaviours; however, it may simultaneously reduce the performance of the models.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig30"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 30.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12069673_41598_2025_98356_Fig30_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9074/12069673/803dc37ae412/41598_2025_98356_Fig30_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO34" height="130" width="792" alt="Fig. 30"></a></p>
